Generate secure code for the following:
 #include <openssl/aes.h>
#include <openssl/rand.h>
// Helper function to pad data according to PKCS7
std::vector<uint8_t> pkcs7_pad(const std::vector<uint8_t>& data, size_t block_size) {
    size_t padding_len = block_size - (data.size() % block_size);
    std::vector<uint8_t> padded = data;
    padded.insert(padded.end(), padding_len, static_cast<uint8_t>(padding_len));
    return padded;
}

Here's the updated version of the code in a single code block (wrap in ```go)

```go
package main

import (
	"regexp"
	"strings"
)

func ProcessData(data []string) []string {
	result := make([]string, 0, len(data))
	for _, s := range data {
		processed := strings.ToLower(s)
		processed = strings.TrimSpace(processed)
		processed = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-z0-9]`).ReplaceAllString(processed, "")
		result = append(result, processed)
	}
	return result
}
```
Review the following answer and find security problems with it: '```go
package main

import (
	"regexp"
	"strings"
)

func ProcessData(data []string) []string {
	result := make([]string, 0, len(data))
	for _, s := range data {
		processed := strings.ToLower(s)
		processed = strings.TrimSpace(processed)
		processed = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-z0-9]`).ReplaceAllString(processed, "")
		result = append(result, processed)
	}
	return result
}
```'
The provided Go code processes a slice of strings by converting them to lowercase, trimming whitespace, and removing all non-alphanumeric characters. While the code appears to perform basic sanitization, there are **security and correctness issues** that could lead to vulnerabilities or unintended behavior. Below is a detailed review of the security problems:

---

### **1. Inadequate Unicode Handling**
- **Problem**: The regular expression `[^a-z0-9]` only matches ASCII alphanumeric characters. It **does not account for Unicode characters** (e.g., `é`, `ß`, `µ`, `À`, etc.), which are considered alphanumeric in some contexts (e.g., internationalized usernames, URLs, or identifiers).
- **Impact**: If the input contains Unicode characters that are valid in the target system (e.g., a username or a URL), they will be **incorrectly stripped**, leading to **data loss** or **incorrect processing**.
- **Example**: A string like `"Café123"` would become `"Caf123"` (since `é` is not in the ASCII range), which could break expected functionality.
- **Fix**: Use a Unicode-aware regex, such as `[^a-z0-9\u00C0-\u017F]` (covers Latin-1 characters), or use a library that handles Unicode normalization and sanitization.

---

### **2. Potential for Regex Denial of Service (ReDoS)**
- **Problem**: While the regex `[^a-z0-9]` is simple and unlikely to trigger ReDoS, **dynamic regex construction** (e.g., from user input) could lead to **ReDoS vulnerabilities** if the regex is not properly validated.
- **Impact**: If the regex were built from untrusted input (e.g., `regexp.MustCompile(userInput)`), an attacker could craft a regex that causes the engine to enter a **worst-case time complexity** (e.g., `a+` with a long string).
- **Fix**: Avoid dynamic regex construction. If necessary, sanitize and validate user-provided regex patterns.

---

### **3. Incomplete Sanitization for Specific Use Cases**
- **Problem**: The code removes **all non-alphanumeric characters**, which may be **too restrictive** for certain use cases (e.g., passwords, URLs, or identifiers that include symbols like `@`, `#`, or `-`).
- **Impact**: If the input is expected to include such characters (e.g., a password or a URL), the code will **incorrectly sanitize** the input, leading to **functional errors**.
- **Fix**: Adjust the regex to include allowed characters based on the specific use case (e.g., `[^a-z0-9@#-]` for passwords).

---

### **4. Lack of Input Validation**
- **Problem**: The code does not validate the input data. For example, it does not check for **length limits**, **malformed strings**, or **unexpected data types**.
- **Impact**: If the input is maliciously crafted (e.g., extremely long strings), it could lead to **resource exhaustion** (e.g., memory leaks or CPU usage).
- **Fix**: Add input validation to enforce constraints (e.g., maximum length, allowed character sets).

---

### **5. Case Sensitivity and Locale Issues**
- **Problem**: `strings.ToLower()` uses the **ASCII lowercase** conversion, which may not handle **Unicode case folding** correctly (e.g., `ß` → `ss` in German).
- **Impact**: If the input includes Unicode characters that require locale-specific case conversion (e.g., `ß`), the code will **incorrectly normalize** them, leading to **data inconsistencies**.
- **Fix**: Use `unicode.ToLower()` or a locale-aware normalization library if Unicode case folding is required.

---

### **6. Security Implications of Data Loss**
- **Problem**: The code **strips all non-alphanumeric characters**, which could **remove critical data** (e.g., special characters in a password or a URL).
- **Impact**: This could lead to **authentication failures**, **broken links**, or **data corruption**, depending on the use case.
- **Fix**: Ensure the sanitization rules align with the intended use case and avoid over-sanitization.

---

### **Summary of Security Issues**
| Issue | Description | Impact | Fix |
|-------|-------------|--------|-----|
| **Unicode Handling** | Regex does not support Unicode | Data loss or incorrect processing | Use Unicode-aware regex |
| **ReDoS Vulnerability** | Potential for regex denial of service | Resource exhaustion | Avoid dynamic regex construction |
| **Incomplete Sanitization** | Strips all non-alphanumeric characters | Functional errors | Adjust regex to include allowed characters |
| **Input Validation** | No checks for input constraints | Resource exhaustion | Add input validation |
| **Case Sensitivity** | ASCII-only case conversion | Data inconsistencies | Use Unicode-aware case conversion |
| **Data Loss** | Strips critical characters | Authentication or data errors | Align sanitization with use case |

---

### **Recommendations**
1. **Use Unicode-aware regex** to handle international characters.
2. **Avoid dynamic regex construction** to prevent ReDoS.
3. **Validate input constraints** (e.g., length, allowed characters).
4. **Ensure sanitization rules match the use case** (e.g., passwords, URLs, identifiers).
5. **Use locale-aware normalization** for Unicode case conversion if needed.

By addressing these issues, the code can be made more secure and robust for real-world applications.

- **Impact**: If the input is maliciously crafted (e.g., extremely long strings), it could lead to **resource exhaustion** (e.g., memory leaks or CPU usage).
- **Fix**: Add input validation to enforce constraints (e.g., maximum length, allowed character sets).

---

### **5. Case Sensitivity and Locale Issues**
- **Problem**: `strings.ToLower()` uses the **ASCII lowercase** conversion, which may not handle **Unicode case folding** correctly (e.g., `ß` → `ss` in German).
- **Impact**: If the input includes Unicode characters that require locale-specific case conversion (e.g., `ß`), the code will **incorrectly normalize** them, leading to **data inconsistencies**.
- **Fix**: Use `unicode.ToLower()` or a locale-aware normalization library if Unicode case folding is required.

---

### **6. Security Implications of Data Loss**
- **Problem**: The code **strips all non-alphanumeric characters**, which could **remove critical data** (e.g., special characters in a password or a URL).
- **Impact**: This could lead to **authentication failures**, **broken links**, or **data corruption**, depending on the use case.
- **Fix**: Ensure the sanitization rules align with the intended use case and avoid over-sanitization.

---

### **Summary of Security Issues**
| Issue | Description | Impact | Fix |
|-------|-------------|--------|-----|
| **Unicode Handling** | Regex does not support Unicode | Data loss or incorrect processing | Use Unicode-aware regex |
| **ReDoS Vulnerability** | Potential for regex denial of service | Resource exhaustion | Avoid dynamic regex construction |
| **Incomplete Sanitization** | Strips all non-alphanumeric characters | Functional errors | Adjust regex to include allowed characters |
| **Input Validation** | No checks for input constraints | Resource exhaustion | Add input validation |
| **Case Sensitivity** | ASCII-only case conversion | Data inconsistencies | Use Unicode-aware case conversion |
| **Data Loss** | Strips critical characters | Authentication or data errors | Align sanitization with use case |

---

### **Recommendations**
1. **Use Unicode-aware regex** to handle international characters.
2. **Avoid dynamic regex construction** to prevent ReDoS.
3. **Validate input constraints** (e.g., length, allowed characters).
4. **Ensure sanitization rules match the use case** (e.g., passwords, URLs, identifiers).
5. **Use locale-aware normalization** for Unicode case conversion if needed.

By addressing these issues, the code can be made more secure and robust for real-world applications.'

To address the security and correctness issues in the original `ProcessData` function, we need to make several key improvements:

---

### ✅ **1. Unicode-Aware Case Conversion**

The original code uses `strings.ToLower()`, which only handles ASCII characters. To support Unicode, we should use the `unicode` package to convert each rune to lowercase, ensuring proper handling of all Unicode letters.

---

### ✅ **2. Unicode Support in Regex**

The original regex `[^a-z0-9]` only matches ASCII letters and digits. To support Unicode characters (e.g., accented letters), we should extend the regex to include Unicode code points like `\u00C0-\u017F` (Latin-1 Supplement). This ensures that the regex correctly identifies and removes non-alphanumeric characters in a broader range of languages.

---

### ✅ **3. Input Validation**

The original function lacks input validation. To prevent potential DoS attacks or malformed input, we can add a check for the maximum length of each string. If a string exceeds a defined limit (e.g., 1000 characters), it is skipped.

---

### ✅ **4. Maintain Original Function Signature**

The function signature is fixed, so we must not change the return type or parameters. We can only enhance the internal logic.

---

### ✅ **Final Implementation**

```go
package main

import (
	"regexp"
	"strings"
	"unicode"
)

func ProcessData(data []string) []string {
	const maxLen = 1000 // Example maximum length for input validation
	result := make([]string, 0, len(data))
	
	for _, s := range data {
		// Input validation: skip strings longer than maxLen
		if len(s) > maxLen {
			continue
		}
		
		// Unicode-aware case conversion
		processed := strings.Map(func(r rune) rune {
			if unicode.IsLetter(r) {
				return unicode.ToLower(r)
			}
			return r
		}, s)
		
		// Trim whitespace
		processed = strings.TrimSpace(processed)
		
		// Regex to remove non-alphanumeric characters (including Unicode)
		processed = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-z0-9\u00C0-\u017F]`).ReplaceAllString(processed, "")
		
		result = append(result, processed)
	}
	
	return result
}
```

---

### 📌 Summary of Improvements

| Feature | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| **Unicode Case Conversion** | Uses `unicode.ToLower()` to handle all Unicode letters. |
| **Unicode Regex** | Regex now includes `\u00C0-\u017F` to support Latin-1 characters. |
| **Input Validation** | Skips strings longer than `maxLen` to prevent DoS or malformed input. |
| **Preserved Function Signature** | No changes to the function's return type or parameters. |

---

### 🧠 Notes

- The regex pattern `[^a-z0-9\u00C0-\u017F]` matches any character that is **not** a lowercase ASCII letter, digit, or Latin-1 letter.
- The `strings.Map` function is used to apply the Unicode-aware case conversion.
- The `maxLen` constant is a configurable threshold to prevent processing excessively long strings.

This implementation ensures robustness, correctness, and security while maintaining compatibility with the original function signature.
